But discourse comprehension researchers would be skeptical of this automatic account. The critical question is not whether related concepts become activated, but whether activation alone is sufficient for building a situation model.

Consider the difference between having two concepts momentarily co-active in working memory versus explicitly constructing the relationship between them. The former might happen automatically through spreading activation; the latter requires goal-directed cognitive work: noticing a gap, generating a bridging inference, and encoding that inference as part of the learned representation. This is the same division of labor that Roelle et al. (2022 Roelle et al. ( , 2023) ) describe as consolidation versus construction. If building broader understanding requires this kind of deliberate inference construction during learning, then self-testing may strengthen memory for individual facts without ever prompting learners to construct connections between them.

If we accept that retrieval practice strengthens retrieval routes for facts, what alternative learning strategy might reliably cause learners to construct connections between them? Explanation reliably achieves this goal, promoting inference generation and conceptual integration (Lombrozo, 2006; McNamara et al., 2023). Explanation can constructively occur during initial exposure and encoding, distinguishing it from retrieval. Extensive research shows that self-explanation (explanation to oneself) causes the learner to infer connections between facts and prior knowledge, building broader understanding and facilitating the learner's ability to generalize the content to new contexts (Aleven & Koedinger, 2002; Chi et al., 1989 Chi et al., , 1994;; Rittle-Johnson, 2006; meta-analysis in Bisra et al., 2018). Theoretical accounts propose that explaining encourages learners to identify causal or structural principles underlying examples, fostering abstraction and transfer (Brockbank & Walker, 2022; Brockbank et al., 2023; Williams & Lombrozo, 2013). When learners self-explain successfully, they build the inferential links that form the backbone of understanding, above and beyond encoding the facts themselves.

In total, the existing literature yields conflicting predictions. Self-testing might foster understanding if retrieval triggers activation that spreads through semantic networks, incidentally strengthening associations among related ideas. Alternatively, if comprehension requires deliberate inference-making during initial encoding, then testing without reading may leave learners able to retrieve facts that they cannot connect. In the current study we use test-only as the operationalization of self-testing: the learner's only encounter with the material is through test questions and corrective feedback, and the source text is never read. The current study therefore addresses two questions. First, can test-only exposure build a situation model, when compared with self-explanation, a strategy designed to promote inference generation and conceptual integration? Second, does adding retrieval practice after test-only exposure change the answer? Experiment 1 addresses the first question. Experiment 2 addresses both, by repeating the learning phase so that its second block requires retrieval of material that participants encoded from feedback in the first block.

Current Study

Across two experiments we examine the effect of a test-only procedure on learners' abilities to make inferences from the tested items. Using a protocol similar to Asher et al. (2025), participants answered multiple-choice questions about facts appearing in educationally realistic materials (i.e., folktales, historical narratives, science textbooks). If they answered incorrectly, they were shown the correct answer and had to mark the correct answer to proceed.

Participants in this condition never saw the source text. In Experiment 2, participants repeated this process a second time, so that the second block involved a retrieval attempt on material they had encoded from feedback. Then, after some delay, we assessed participants' memory for the facts by presenting the same multiple-choice fact questions, and we also assessed participants' performance on novel multiple-choice inference questions. Inference questions were carefully authored to evaluate participants' ability to make an elaborative inference that bridges two fact questions, such that a learner who understood the answers to the fact questions should, in principle, be able to infer the correct answer to the corresponding inference question.

The current study is use-inspired, meaning that it examines learning conditions designed to reflect real educational practice. The conditions we compare are therefore practices, not controlled manipulations of a single variable. The test-only and self-explaining conditions differ in whether participants see the source text, in what cognitive task participants perform, and in how much time participants spend. These differences are properties of the practices themselves rather than confounds to be eliminated, and the difference in time on task is itself one of the practical results we report below.

The first experiment makes a largely within-subjects comparison between test-only learning and self-explaining. During self-explaining trials, participants were shown the educational source materials as text passages and were prompted after each paragraph to explain the text in their own words. The text included the fact statements, but did not include the target inferences, so that participants would still need to generate elaborative inferences to answer the inference questions correctly. This first exploratory experiment was conducted in a single session.

The second experiment (preregistered and confirmatory) was designed to replicate and extend the results of the first experiment. The second experiment included the same test-only and self-explaining conditions, and introduced a third condition, reading, where participants merely read a text passage of the source material during the learning phase, without testing items or self-explanation prompts. The second experiment was conducted in two separate sessions, with a minimum 24-hour delay between the learning phase and the test phase. The purpose of the delay was to maximize the potential effect of testing, which becomes more pronounced after a retention interval greater than one day (Rowland, 2014; Toppino & Cohen, 2009). Critically, during Experiment 2's learning session, participants repeated the learning phase in two blocks. The purpose of this repetition was to facilitate retrieval practice on top of test-only exposure: the first block is the learner's initial encounter with the material, and the second block invites participants in the test-only condition to retrieve from memory the answers they encoded from feedback in the first block. By the end of the second learning block, participants in the test-only condition reached 86% accuracy on fact questions, well within the range that the retrieval practice literature considers a meaningful level of retrieval success (Adesope et al., 2017; Rowland, 2014). Experiment 2 therefore provides a strong test of retrieval practice itself, even though the condition as a whole remains test-only.

In Experiment 2, we also collected baseline performance measures on the inference questions from a sample of participants who had no prior exposure to the learning materials.

Finally, we conducted exploratory analyses of participants' self-explanations across the two experiments combined, coding for whether there was evidence of inferences within participants' self-explanations, and the association between these inferences and participants' performance on subsequent test items.

Experiment 1

The first experiment was conducted online with college-aged volunteer research participants and was exploratory. All materials, analysis scripts, and data are openly available (Motz, 2025a).

Method

Participants

We recruited 254 participants, who each received credit for partial fulfillment of a course requirement in Introductory Psychology at a large public university in the midwestern US. All participants provided informed consent. There were no exclusions; all participants who completed the session are included in the current analysis. Participants self-reported an average age of 18.8 years (SD = 1.4). Gender identities were 73.6% female, 22.8% male, and 3.6% other or non-disclosed. Participants were 68.9% White, 11.8% Asian, and 7.1% African American or Black, and separately, 9.1% reported Hispanic or Latin ethnicity. Experiment 1 was exploratory and we set no target sample size in advance. The study was made available to the subject pool for the whole of the academic term; 254 volunteers signed up and completed the session. A sensitivity analysis for this design is reported below, alongside the corresponding analysis for Experiment 2.

Materials

Source material consisted of nine text passages: three folktales, three historical narratives, and three science texts. Folktales were from Waddill (1992), historical narratives Table 1. Example materials from a folktale (top row), historical narrative (middle row), and science text (bottom row).

Source Text

Fact Questions Inference Question …Spider thought Tortoise was a nuisance and decided to be mean to him. So Spider told all the animals that they had to wash their dirty hands before eating. All of the animals went to do as Spider had said. There was a path that went through the forest. Tortoise went to the stream and washed. But he had a problem after that. As he walked back, his hands got dirty again.

Tortoise tried over and over, but he couldn't walk any other way. So he never got to eat at Spider's table… What did Spider require of his guests? (Answer: They wash their hands before sitting down and eating) Why couldn't Tortoise get anything to eat at Spider's feast? (Answer: He could not follow Spider's rules) What problem did Tortoise have when going to Spider's feast? (Answer: When he would walk back to the feast after washing his hands they would get dirty again) Zahir al-Din Muhammad Babur had always dreamed of founding a great empire. His father, the ruler of a small central Asian state named Fergana in what is now Uzbekistan, was a descendant of the famous conqueror Timur. His mother came from the family of the Mongol leader Chinggis Khan… In 1504, he made a bold move. Striking out across the Hindu Kush mountains accompanied only by his family and two hundred fighting men, he conquered the city of Kabul in Afghanistan … Who were the notable ancestors of Babur? (Answer: The famous conqueror Timur and leader of the Mongol empire Chinggis Khan) How did Babur follow in his ancestors' footsteps? (Answer: He conquered Central and Southern Asian territories and established an empire) What bold decision did Babur make as ruler of Fergana? (Answer: To conquer the city of Kabul in Afghanistan) …The kidneys are involved in maintaining the water level in the body, and regulating red blood cell levels and blood pressure.

They react to changes in the body's water level throughout the day. As water intake decreases, the kidneys adjust accordingly and leave water in the body instead of helping remove it through the urine. The kidneys need constant pressure to filter the blood. When the blood pressure drops too low, the kidneys increase the pressure by producing angiotensin, a blood vesselconstricting protein.

When do the kidneys produce angiotensin? (Answer: When the blood pressure is too low) What does constricting the blood vessels do? (Answer: Increases the blood pressure) What is angiotensin? (Answer: A blood vessel-constricting hormone) were adapted from the LibreTexts open library (libretexts.org) and Khan Academy (khanacademy.org), and science texts were adapted from McNamara et al. (2023) and OpenStax (openstax.org).

For each of these nine text passages, we composed four inference questions and eight fact questions. The answers to inference questions were not directly provided in the texts. For a reader to know the answer to an inference question, they would need to build a conceptual bridge between two separate sentences, and the constituent facts from these two sentences were included as fact questions (see Table 1). Thus, importantly, someone who knows the answers to each of the 8 fact questions should have all necessary information to infer the correct answers to the 4 inference questions.

Procedure

There were two phases, learning and test, and there were two blocks within the test phase: memory test block, and inference test block. The order of the memory and inference test blocks was randomized. The full experiment was performed in a single online session (see Figure 1), and was scripted using jsPsych (de Leeuw et al., 2023).

Learning Phase. During the learning phase, participants learned material from one randomly sampled folktale, historical narrative, and science text (three sets of material total), in a random order. For each of these, the content was presented on a single page, and participants were exposed to the material in one of two different learning conditions: test-only or self-explaining (see screenshots in Figure 2). Participants were explicitly instructed not to write down nor copy anything in the experiment, as the purpose of the experiment was to measure what they could remember.

We randomized learning condition independently for each passage, and test block order independently of that, rather than constraining assignment to produce equal cell sizes. In effect, participants could be assigned to either condition for one, two, or all three sets of learning material during the learning phase. This provided a more robust comparison between conditions, where the "dosage" of an experimental treatment was permitted to vary across the sample. The cost of this variability is unequal cell sizes, which our mixed-effects models accommodate through the random intercept for each participant. In Experiment 1 this produced the following assignment: 33 participants received the test-only condition for none of their three passages, 99 for one, 84 for two, and 38 for all three. Test block order was likewise randomized per participant, giving 146 participants who received the inference block first and 108 who received the memory block first. The resulting analysis cells contain 94 and 122 participants for the self-explaining condition (fact and inference tests, respectively) and 90 and 131 for the testonly condition.

In the test-only condition, participants responded to eight multiple-choice fact questions (each with four response options), and received feedback on their responses, but never saw the original source text. Barring any prior knowledge about the passage, participants in the test-only condition would be guessing. The eight fact questions were always presented in the order that the facts appeared in the text; in effect, the two fact questions corresponding to an inference question were presented consecutively. Inference questions were never shown in the learning phase. To enforce the order that participants responded to the questions, each participant began with the first question shown, with all remaining questions blurred and disabled. The order of response options was randomized. Participants clicked to select a response, and then clicked a button to submit their answer. If they answered correctly, they received feedback ("Correct"), the answer button became disabled, and the next question became visible with its answer button enabled. If they responded incorrectly, they received feedback ("The correct answer is: [answer]"), and then had to mark the correct answer to proceed. Notably, this extra step extends the procedure described in Asher et al. (2025), which did not require participants to mark the correct answer to proceed. After responding to the eight fact questions, participants were shown a text box with the instruction, "Summarize the main ideas in the questions and answers above," and if participants provided a passable summary (see criteria in the self-explanation condition, below), then they continued along in the session. By asking participants to summarize the main ideas in the test-only condition, we actively encourage participants to make inferences, and thus avoid the possibility that participants would have made inferences in the test-only condition had only they been instructed to do so.

In the self-explaining condition, participants read the original source text of the learning content, and after each paragraph, they typed a response to the prompt, "Explain the text in your own words." The web interface prevented participants from selecting or copying the source text (using the cascading style sheet property, "user-select: none"), requiring them to read the source text and manually type their response. Analogous to the test-only condition, each participant began with the first paragraph visible, with all remaining paragraphs blurred.

Participants had to type a passable explanation of the paragraph to proceed. Self-explanations were evaluated against two minimal criteria: (1) the length of the explanation had to be more than 4 words, and more than 10 characters total, and (2) the cosine similarity between the explanation and the original source text had to be ≥ 80% (this second criterion also entailed a requirement that the self-explanation contain at least one English word, to calculate similarity). These minimal criteria were not intended to enforce a good explanation, but rather to enforce a Figure 1. Experiment 1 procedure. During the learning phase, participants were randomly assigned to a topic order (Science, then History, then Folktale in the example shown), and each topic was randomly assigned to a condition (either test-only or self-explaining). During the test phase, participants were randomly assigned to a block order; and they could perform the memory test block first (as shown in the example above) or the inference test block first.

passable explanation, merely ensuring basic compliance with the task. The second criterion was evaluated using word2vec embeddings for 10,000 of the most common English words along 300 dimensions (Mikolov et al., 2013). We calculated the average vector of all words in the participant's response, and the average vector of all words in the corresponding source text paragraph, independent of their order. Because averaging word vectors in this way discards word order and most of the information about specific word choice, the resulting score measures topical overlap rather than verbatim reuse. A response that shares few surface words with the source paragraph can still score highly, provided it is about the same content, which is why the criterion is compatible with the instruction to explain the text in one's own words. If the cosine similarity between these average vectors was below 80%, participants received the feedback, "Your summary is missing some key elements. Please be sure to summarize the main ideas in your own words." Upon providing a passable response that met both criteria, the next paragraph became visible, or (if it was the last paragraph of a passage) a button to proceed became enabled.

Memory Test Block.

During the memory test block, participants responded to the eight fact questions from the randomly sampled set of learning materials (one folktale, one historical narrative, and one science text), in the same random order as presented in the learning phase (24 items total). If participants had been assigned to the test-only condition for one of these, the memory test was a repetition of the exact same items for that set. If participants had been assigned to the self-explaining condition for one of these, the memory test items for the set would have been unfamiliar. Because question sets were presented in the same order as presented in the learning phase, there was necessarily a brief delay between learning and test for any single set while participants worked through the intervening two sets.

In this phase, all eight multiple-choice questions for each set of learning materials were presented on a single page, identical to the test-only condition, in the same order, but with three key modifications: no feedback was provided, participants could proceed to the next item no matter their response, and there was no prompt to summarize.

Inference Test Block. During the inference test block, participants responded to the four inference questions from the randomly sampled learning materials (one folktale, one historical narrative, and one science text), again in the same random order as presented in the learning phase. Each inference question involved an elaborative inference between precisely two fact questions, and the inference questions appeared in the same order as their corresponding fact questions would have appeared in the test-only condition or in the memory test phase.

The inference questions were multiple-choice and were presented on a single page, with no feedback. Participants could proceed to the next item no matter their response.

Statistical Analysis

Data from the current experiment were analyzed using a mixed-effects linear model, with parameters estimated in a Bayesian framework, using brms (Bürkner, 2017), with random intercepts for each participant. We used the default priors in brms; for experimental effects, these were flat priors with uniform density across the range of possible values, appropriate for an exploratory study. Model parameters are estimated across 3 chains and 2,000 iterations. The effective sample sizes (ESS) of all reported estimates exceeded 1,100 (bulk) and 1,400 (tail), and potential scale reduction factors (𝑅 ) are ≤ 1.01. If the 95% credible interval of an effect's posterior estimate excludes zero and values close to zero, then we can infer a credible effect.

Learning condition varied within participants for most of the sample (n=183), but 71 of 254 participants (28%) in Experiment 1 experienced only one learning condition (38 test-only for all three passages and 33 self-explaining for all three). The random intercept for individual participants accommodates this mix of within-and between-participant comparisons. Question type is treated as a between-participant factor in every analysis of test performance reported below, because each of those analyses is restricted to a participant's first test block, and block order was randomized between participants. The comparison of inference performance with and without a preceding fact retrieval test is likewise between participants, since each participant answered the inference questions in one test block only. Full model specifications and posterior estimates appear in Table 2 for Experiment 1 and Table 3 for Experiment 2.

Most of the estimated coefficients in the current experiment are reported on a log-odds scale, representing the logarithm of the change in probability of the participant answering a question correctly, where positive values indicate that the event is more likely, and negative values indicate that the event is less likely. Log-odds have the advantage of providing a scale centered around zero (whereas odds and probabilities cannot be negative), but because they are on a logarithm scale, log-odds estimates are not directly interpretable on the scale of the data.

Results and Discussion

Learning Phase

Participants took a median of 18.6 minutes to complete the learning phase. As expected, for any single set of materials, participants were faster to complete the test-only task (median = 3.5 minutes; where participants marked correct answers) than the self-explaining task (median = 6.0 minutes; where participants typed passable responses). The test-only task therefore took roughly 60% of the time that self-explaining took, an efficiency difference we return to in the General Discussion.

Test-Only. During test-only learning, participants answered eight multiple-choice fact questions with feedback. All fact questions had four response options, only one of which was correct, so the odds of responding correctly by chance were 25%. However, participants' average accuracy on their first response to fact questions was 35.7% (SD = 47.9%), indicating that they possessed some relevant prior knowledge or common sense. The average accuracy on first response was 38.3% for science texts, 36.0% for folktales, and 33.1% for historical narratives. Participants were credibly more accurate in their first response on science questions than historical narratives (estimated accuracy difference in log odds = 0.24; 95% CI: 0.05 to 0.42), which likely reflects some familiarity with individual science facts from prior coursework, rather than richer situational understanding of the science content.

If a participant responded incorrectly, they were told the correct answer, and then they had to select the correct answer to proceed to the next question. Thus, if a participant responded incorrectly on their first response, their average accuracy on their second response (after being told the correct answer), was at ceiling, 97.9% on average.

Self-Explaining.

During self-explaining, participants read a series of paragraphs, and after each paragraph, they were prompted to explain the text in their own words (see Figure 2).

Participants' explanations were minimally evaluated for their length and the similarity between the explanation and the source paragraph (at least 80% similarity).

Combining all self-explanations submitted in the learning phase, 93.6% of participants' submissions passed, 5.8% had low similarity to the source text, and 0.01% were too short. Of the participants who were assigned to the self-explaining task at least once (n = 216), 70.4% passed on every opportunity, and never had to revise a submitted explanation.

Passing explanations had an average length of 267.7 characters (SD = 140.3; about 54 words) overall; 253.7 for science texts, 276.8 for folktales, and 273.1 for historical narratives.

Participants provided shorter explanations for science texts compared with folktales (estimate: 31.2 fewer characters; 95% CI: 12.5 to 50.6) and compared with historical narratives (estimate: 31.7 fewer characters; 95% CI: 12.8 to 51.0).

On average, passing explanations had 91.6% similarity (SD = 4.5%) to the source text, which is an index of semantic similarity and not merely word overlap. There were no credible differences in similarity between topics.

Comparing Immediate Test Performance Between Test-Only and Self-Explaining

Immediately after the learning phase, some participants were randomly assigned to the memory test block (where they answered fact questions; n = 108; 42.5%), while others were randomly assigned to the inference test block (where they answered inference questions; n = 146; 57.5%). Here, we isolate our analysis only to this second block of the experiment (either memory or inference), which is the first of the two testing blocks, with full model statistics reported in Table 2, Model A. Overall, averaging across question types, there was no credible difference in the logodds of answering a test question correctly between test-only and self-explaining conditions (estimate: 0.03; 95% CI: -0.15 to 0.22). And when averaging across conditions, participants performed better on fact questions than on inference questions (estimate: 0.84; 95% CI: 0.56 to 1.12). However, we observe a strong crossover interaction between question type and condition (estimate: 1.47; 95% CI: 1.12 to 1.83): participants answered fact-based questions more accurately when they had studied the text in the test-only condition, but they answered inference questions more accurately when they had processed the text using self-explanation. This interaction is visualized in Figure 3. Model-estimated accuracy (posterior means with 95% CIs) was 89.4% (86.9 to 91.8%) on fact questions and 63.9% (58.8 to 68.8%) on inference questions for material learned in the test-only condition, and 80.8% (77.1 to 84.3%) on fact entries are model coefficients, so each is expressed relative to the reference cell noted in parentheses. Each coefficient is a simple effect: main-effect rows give the contrast at the reference level of the other factor, and interaction rows give the difference between those simple effects. The comparisons reported in the main text are estimated marginal means and contrasts derived from these same posteriors, averaged over the remaining factor (and in places transformed to the response scale), so their values and signs will not correspond to individual coefficients in these tables.

questions and 79.2% (75.0 to 82.9%) on inference questions for material learned by selfexplaining.

Pairwise contrast confirms that for fact questions, participants performed credibly better when they were assigned to the test-only condition compared with self-explaining (estimate: 0.70; 95% CI: 0.45 to 0.97). This is not particularly surprising, because participants were given the correct answers to fact questions in the test-only condition. Nevertheless, it demonstrates that learning occurred in the test-only condition: during the learning phase, participants' average first response accuracy on fact question was 35.7%, but accuracy during the subsequent test phase increased to 84.6%. This was across an average retention interval of 8.24 minutes (the median duration between completing the test-only task in the learning phase with a given set of materials and then being tested on the same materials in the next testing phase).

Pairwise contrast also confirms that for inference questions, participants performed credibly worse when they learned in the test-only condition compared with self-explaining (estimate = -0.77; 95% CI: -1.02 to -0.50). It is noteworthy here that the answers to inference questions were never provided during the learning phase. Further, each inference question was carefully designed as an inference bridging two fact questions -and despite demonstrating better memory for fact questions, participants who learned in the test-only condition had worse performance at making the relevant inferences. This was also despite an explicit instruction, in the test-only condition's learning phase, to summarize the main ideas in the multiple-choice questions and answers.

Does Retrieval Improve Inferences?

Some participants answered inference questions immediately after the learning phase, but others answered fact questions and then answered inference questions in the final block of the session. We might ask whether there were improvements in participants' ability to make accurate inferences in the third block, compared with inference performance in the second block. Such an effect would be expected if retrieval practice, retrieving the items from memory in the second block, improved participants' ability to make inferences.

However, we find no evidence that retrieval (answering multiple-choice fact questions during the second block) improved participants' ability to make inferences during the third block, compared with inference performance during the second block (estimate: 0.08; 95% CI: -0.19 to 0.37). Further, there was no credible interaction effect in inference performance between these two blocks for material that was initially learned in the test-only condition vs. for material learned by self-explaining (estimate: 0.36; 95% CI: -0.04 to 0.74). Full model statistics reported in Table 2, Model B.

Differences Between Learning Materials

We conducted an exploratory analysis of whether the difference between the test-only and self-explaining conditions varied across the three kinds of learning material. Overall performance was highest for folktales, intermediate for historical narratives, and lowest for science texts, and these marginal differences were qualified by a three-way interaction between question type, condition, and topic. The self-explaining advantage on inference questions held for folktales and historical narratives and was absent for science texts. Test performance on science fact questions was also worse than on the other topics, despite participants beginning the learning phase with their highest first-response accuracy on science questions. Because the passages were not designed to be equivalent on dimensions other than domain, we treat these differences as exploratory and report the full statistics in the Appendix. We return to the science text result in the Combined Analyses below, where we examine whether participants showed evidence of generated inferences in their self-explanations.

Summary

In Experiment 1, participants learned educationally realistic materials either in the testonly condition (answering fact questions with corrective feedback), or by reading and selfexplaining the materials in their own words. We then assessed participants' memory for facts from these materials and their ability to make inferences bridging the facts. Participants had better memory for facts when they learned in the test-only condition, which was expected because participants in the test-only condition had been informed about the correct answers.

But despite this advantage, participants who learned in the test-only condition had credibly lower performance on inference questions than participants who learned by self-explaining.

These findings challenge recent claims that robust learning might emerge from testing alone, in the absence of initial exposure to the material. While test-only learning can aid learners' ability to remember facts, this advantage does not necessarily translate to broader understanding.

An additional retrieval step in the second block, when participants attempted to retrieve the facts from memory without feedback, did not improve participants' performance on inference questions in the third block. This null finding might be interpreted to suggest that any benefits of retrieval practice do not operate through elaborative encoding mechanisms, because practicing retrieval of facts from memory did not improve participants' ability to make connections between these facts (see also Karpicke & Smith, 2012). However, robust benefits of retrieval practice are more often observed after longer retention intervals (over 24 hours) than those employed in Experiment 1 (a few minutes), so Experiment 1 does not provide a fair test of the potential benefit of retrieval practice. This issue is corrected in Experiment 2, which was conducted in two sessions with a minimum 24-hour delay between learning and test.

The differences in learning performance between test-only and self-explaining did not generalize across all learning materials. For folktales and historical narratives, learning by selfexplaining yielded credibly higher performance on inference questions than learning in the testonly condition, but this was not observed for science topics. For science topics, there was no difference in participants' accuracy on inference questions between test-only and selfexplaining. This cannot simply be explained by low prior knowledge about science, because on the contrary, participants' initial responses on science fact questions were more accurate than the fact questions about the other materials. Despite this initial advantage for science material, learning in the test-only condition yielded lower performance in the test phase on science questions, suggesting that the science facts were harder to learn than the history facts and folktale facts. This difficulty may have also hampered inference performance on science topics in the self-explaining condition. Notably, recent claims of the effectiveness of testing alone were based on studies where participants learned statistics (Asher et al., 2025) and the geology of Yellowstone (Carvalho et al., 2024), which lacked narrative structure and were perhaps most similar to science material in the current study. In Combined Analyses, below, we examine participants' written self-explanations to test for differences between material in whether participants made inferences during the learning phase.

There were two additional deficiencies in the design of Experiment 1. First, Experiment 1 lacked a true control condition. We observed that test-only learning is less effective at building broader understanding than self-explaining, but it remains possible that test-only learning could still improve broader understanding better than some other minimally effective comparison condition. Thus, we lack evidence to claim that test-only learning is ineffective at building broader understanding. Second, we lack baseline data on participants' performance on inference questions, in the absence of any learning. While unlikely, it remains possible that participants had no improvement in their ability to make inferences at all, no matter their learning condition. To address these issues, Experiment 2 includes a third condition, reading, where participants merely read a text passage of the source material during the learning phase, without testing items or self-explanation prompts. Furthermore, in parallel to Experiment 2, we collected baseline performance data on the inference questions from a secondary sample of naïve participants without any exposure to learning material.

Experiment 2

Experiment 2 was confirmatory; directional hypotheses and an analysis plan were publicly preregistered (Motz, 2025b), and we do not deviate from the preregistration. Experiment 2 differed from Experiment 1 in three prominent ways: first, we introduced a new learning condition, reading, where participants merely read the text passages without any prompts to self-explain; second, we conducted Experiment 2 in two sessions, imposing a minimum 24-hour delay between the learning and test phases; and third, participants repeated the learning phase in two blocks. This repetition was the central design manipulation of Experiment 2. Block 1 is the test-only encounter with the material, and block 2 invites participants in the test-only condition to retrieve the answers from memory, prior to the delay.

It is worth situating the test-only procedure within the broader cognitive psychology tradition of retrieval practice. Classic retrieval practice experiments use word pairs: participants briefly view pairs during a study phase, then practice retrieving one word given the other (e.g., Carpenter, 2009; Hogan & Kintsch, 1971; Izawa, 1967; Karpicke & Smith, 2012; McDaniel & Masson, 1985). The initial study phase in these experiments is minimal by design, and in some cases, participants are explicitly instructed to spend as little time as possible on study items (Karpicke, 2009; Koriat, Ma'ayan, & Nussinson, 2006). Experiment 2's two-block learning phase has a similar design: the first block supplies a brief encounter with the correct answers through feedback, and the second block requires retrieval of that content. If retrieval itself builds broader understanding, then the test-only condition in Experiment 2 should close the inference gap that we observed in Experiment 1.

In preregistering Experiment 2, we hypothesized that (1) test-only learning will yield better memory for facts than self-explaining and reading, (2) self-explaining will yield better inference performance than test-only and reading, and thus (3) there will be a significant interaction between condition and question type. Moreover, we hypothesized that (4) there will be differences in the above effects for the different topics (folktales, history, science), such that the fact advantage for test-only learning will be reduced for folktales, and the self-explaining advantage for inferences will be reduced for science texts. This is because science texts tend to lack the narrative structure and conceptual gaps that naturally prompt inference-making during self-explanation, and without explicit comprehension strategy training, most readers are unlikely to generate the bridging inferences that build a situation model (McCarthy et al., 2018; McNamara, 2004 McNamara, , 2017;; Thorndyke, 1977).

Finally, Experiment 2 also included a small secondary sample of participants who merely answered the inference questions without feedback. This allows us to estimate baseline performance on inference questions in the absence of any training. This secondary sample was not preregistered, it is not used for any of the preregistered analyses, and it is only intended to provide a rough benchmark on inference performance among naïve participants (shown in Figure 5, below).

All materials, data, and analysis scripts from Experiment 2 are openly available at (Motz, 2025a).

Method

Participants

We recruited participants for a two-session online experiment from the Introductory Psychology subject pool at a large public university in the midwestern US (same as Experiment 1). Any participant who had signed up for Experiment 1 in the previous semester was restricted from signing up for Experiment 2. All participants provided informed consent. Initially, 91 participants signed up for and participated in Experiment 2's first session (learning phase), but only 68 participants (74.7%) returned and completed the second session (test phase) within the time limit. There were no additional exclusions; all 68 participants who completed both sessions are included in the current analysis. Participants self-reported an average age of 19.3 years (SD = 1.2 years), and gender identities that were 63.2% female, 35.3% male, and 1.5% other or non-disclosed. Participants were 70.6% White, 20.6% Asian, and 1.5% African American or Black, and separately, 4.4% reported Hispanic or Latin ethnicity.

Our preregistered stopping rule was to conclude data collection after 250 participants or at the end of the academic term, and we obeyed this stopping rule despite having collected completed data from only 68 participants at the end of the term. To quantify the power of his sample, we conducted a simulation-based sensitivity analysis, which asks how large an effect this design can reliably detect. Holding the design at its realized form (68 participants, the 39 to 29 split between the two test block orders, three within-participant learning conditions, and the estimated between-participant variability), we generated data from the fitted model while varying the size of the condition × question type crossover effect, and refit the model to each simulated dataset (175 replicates). We define that crossover as the difference between the self-explaining and test-only conditions on inference questions minus the same difference on fact questions, which is the quantity our central prediction concerns. With a sample size of 68 participants, experiment 2 recovers this crossover with 80% probability once it reaches 1.13 in log-odds, corresponding to a difference of differences of about 11 percentage points on the response scale. The crossover we actually observed in Experiment 2 was 2.22 (95% CI: 1.45 to 3.03), which this design recovers with probability greater than .99. Applying the same procedure to Experiment 1 gives a smallest reliably detectable crossover of 0.50 in log-odds, so the minimal detectable effect in Experiment 2 is roughly 2.3 times larger than Experiment 1, but still smaller than the empirical finding. Across all replicates the maximum potential scale reduction factor was 1.02 and no divergent transitions occurred. The simulation script is available in the project repository (Motz, 2025a).

Secondary Sample.

Separate from the sample described above, we recruited a small secondary sample of 27 naïve participants from the same subject pool, who had not participated in any version of the current study. This sample was recruited to provide a rough exploratory estimate of baseline accuracy on multiple-choice inference questions, shown in Figure 5 (and not included in any other analyses). We did not collect demographic data from participants in this secondary sample, but we have no reason to believe it differs from the demographic properties of the larger samples in Experiments 1 and 2.

Materials

The materials from Experiment 1 were used for Experiment 2 without modification.

Procedure

Experiment 2 was conducted in two separate online sessions; the first session was a learning phase and the second session was a testing phase. The second session became available to participants exactly 24 hours after they completed the first session. Participants received email reminders to complete the second session over the following week, and their access to the second session expired 192 hours (8 days) after they completed the first session.

On average, participants completed the second session 63.9 hours (2.7 days; SD = 53.3 hours) after completing the first session. The procedure for Experiment 2 is shown in Figure 4. During the learning phase (session 1), participants were randomly assigned to a topic order (Science, then History, then Folktale in the example shown), and the three conditions (test-only, self-explaining, or reading) were randomly assigned to topics. This learning phase was conducted in two identical blocks. During the test phase (session 2; minimum 24 hours after session 1), participants were randomly assigned to a block order; they could perform the memory test block first (as shown in the example above) or the inference test block first.

Learning Phase. The learning phase was similar to Experiment 1, but with two modifications. First, we introduced a new learning condition, reading. In the reading condition, participants merely read the text passage paragraph-by-paragraph. The instructions were to "read the text carefully" and "try to remember the information in the text as best you can."

Initially only the first paragraph was visible, and the remaining paragraphs were blurred. After each paragraph, participants pressed a button to proceed, which caused the next paragraph to become visible. There was no prompt to summarize at the end of the passage in the reading condition.

The second modification to the learning phase was that it was repeated in two successive blocks. In the first block, participants were assigned to one randomly selected folktale, historical narrative, and science text, and these were randomly assigned to one of three learning conditions: test-only, self-explanation, or reading. Because there were now three learning conditions in Experiment 2 (matching the number of to-be-learned sets of materials), we constrained our randomization so that participants performed all three conditions across the three sets of materials in random order, yielding a balanced within-subject design. After they completed the first block, participants then repeated the same procedure with the same materials a second time. We told participants that this repetition was designed to help them improve their memory for the material. The reading and the self-explaining conditions were identical between the two blocks. In the test-only condition, the item order was the same between the two blocks, but the order of the response options within each question was reshuffled in the second block, so that the position of the correct answer varied between the two blocks. Also, the test-only condition only prompted participants to explain the main topics in the first block, not the second block. Participants in the test-only condition received correct answer feedback and had to mark the correct answer before they could proceed in both blocks of the learning phase.

Test Phase. After a delay of 24-192 hours following the completion of the learning phase, participants returned to complete the test phase in a second online session. The test phase was identical to Experiment 1; again, there were two blocks: memory test block and inference test block, and the order of these blocks were individually randomized (39 participants received the memory block first, 29 the inference block first). Within each block, participants responded to questions about their assigned materials from the first session, in the same order that they had been presented in the first session. If a participant had been assigned to the testonly condition for a set of materials in the learning phase, the memory questions were identical to those that had previously been shown. Inference questions were novel to all participants. No feedback was shown during the test phase.

Baseline Inference Test Performance. Participants recruited in the secondary sample (n = 27) answered the full set of all inference test questions in a single online session. There were 9 sets of inference questions (3 folktale, 3 history, and 3 science), with 4 questions within each set. The 9 sets were presented in random order (shuffled separately for each participant), but the order of questions within each set were kept fixed, identical to the item order presented in the test phase.

Statistical Analysis

In Experiment 2 we used the same analytical approach as Experiment 1, and the same flat default priors. The within-and between-participant structure is similar, except that learning condition is fully within participants (every participant experienced all three conditions), whereas question type and test block order vary between participants. Model specifications and posterior estimates are reported in Table 3.

Results and Discussion

Learning Phase

Participants took a median duration of 23.3 minutes to complete the learning phase, and took about twice as long to complete the first block (median = 15.0 minutes) than the second block (median = 7.1 minutes). In block 1, participants spent more time self-explaining (median = 7.1 minutes) than test-only learning (median = 3.6 minutes) and reading (median = 1.1 minutes).

This pattern repeated in block 2, where the median time was 4.6 minutes for self-explaining, 0.6 minutes for test-only learning, and 0.1 minutes reading. Given that a typical participant only spent 0.1 minutes in the reading condition in the second learning block (about 6 seconds), we infer that they were merely skimming the text passages. The test-only task was therefore markedly more efficient than self-explaining in both blocks, and the gap widened with repetition, since repeating a set of questions took far less time than repeating a written explanation.

Test-Only. In the first block of the learning phase, participants' average accuracy of first response to fact questions was 42.1% (SD = 20.6%), well above chance accuracy of 25%, again revealing some prior knowledge and/or common sense. The average accuracy on first response was 49.2% for science texts, 40.1% for folktales, and 39.7% for historical narratives, however these differences are not credibly different from one another.

When participants repeated the same multiple-choice questions in the second block, average accuracy on their first response rose to 86.2% (SD = 16.3%), an estimated increase in log-odds of answering correctly of 1.01 (95% CI: 0.51 to 1.56). This is very close to the average accuracy observed in Experiment 1 in the memory test (84.6%), which is fitting considering that the memory test in Experiment 1 is analogous to the test-only condition's second block during the learning phase of Experiment 2.

As was the case between the learning and test phases in Experiment 1, we also see an inversion in relative performance between the different topics from block 1 to block 2 in Experiment 2's learning phase. In the second block, the average accuracy on first response rose to 71.9% for science texts, 93.5% for folktales, and 87.0% for historical narratives.

Improvement was credibly smaller for science questions than for folktale and history questions (estimated interaction in log-odds: 1.79; 95% CI: 1.13 to 2.44), and furthermore, in block 2, average accuracy on first response to folktale and history questions was credibly higher than accuracy on science questions (estimated difference in log-odds: 1.39; 95% CI: 0.82 to 1.97), directionally opposite the pattern observed in block 1. As with Experiment 1, this is taken as evidence that the science material was less memorable than the folktale and history material.

Even though participants showed higher initial accuracy on science fact questions, which suggesting some familiarity with isolated science facts, this familiarity is narrow. Unlike folktales and historical narratives, where participants bring world knowledge and narrative schemas that support comprehension and inference, science content may lack the kind of integrated situational understanding that facilitates learning new facts and concepts.

Self-Explaining.

In block 1 of the learning phase, 95.9% of participants' selfexplanations passed, 3.5% had low similarity to the source text, and 0.01% were too short.

During block 1, 85.3% of participants submitted passable self-explanations on every prompt, never having to revise a submitted explanation. Passing explanations had an average length of 304.6 characters (SD = 162.6; about 75 words) overall; 266.0 for science texts, 358.5 for folktales, and 306.7 for historical narratives. Participants provided shorter explanations for science texts compared with folktales (102.5 fewer characters; 95% CI: 27.1 to 178.8), but the difference between science texts and historical narratives was not credibly different from zero (42.2 fewer characters; 95% CI: -31.4 to 114.0).

When participants repeated the self-explanation task in block 2, there was a small but reliable overall decrease in the log-odds that a participant would submit a passing selfexplanation (-1.90; 95% CI: -3.8 to -0.43), but this decrease was entirely isolated to science texts. At the participant-level, the average probability of generating a passable self-explanation of a science text decreased from 98.4% in block 1 to 91.9% in block 2, while participants became directionally more likely to submit a passable self-explanation of folktales and historical narratives (interaction effect estimate in log-odds: -13.63; 95% CI: -50.65 to -2.66). There was no credible change in the length of participants' self-explanations between the two blocks, neither overall nor for specific topics. Rather, self-explanations of science texts were less likely to pass in block 2 because they were missing key aspects of the source text (and were scored beneath the similarity threshold).

Reading. In the reading condition, there was no behavioral task; participants clicked to reveal the subsequent paragraph at their discretion. As discussed previously, participants in the reading condition spent roughly 1.1 minutes reading each passage during block 1 of the learning phase and merely skimmed the passages during block 2. There were no credible differences in reading time between topics, nor topic by block interactions.

Averaging across question types, the log-odds of correctly answering a test question were credibly higher for material that was learned by self-explaining than reading (0.87; 95% CI: 0.49 to 1.22), and credibly higher for material that was learned in the test-only condition than reading (1.06; 95% CI: 0.67 to 1.44). In these estimated marginal means, there was no credible difference in performance between self-explaining and test-only learning (0.20; 95% CI: -0.19 to 0.63).

Again, there were strong interaction effects between condition and question type, visualized in Figure 5. Model-estimated accuracy (posterior means with 95% CIs) on fact inferences in the second block of the test session, compared with inference performance in the first block of the test session (without an additional explicit effort at fact retrieval). This is a mixed-effect comparison. It contrasts the participants who answered the inference questions in the first test block against those who answered them in the second block, after a fact retrieval step. Learning condition (test-only, self-explaining, reading) remains a within-participant factor. Averaging across learning conditions, inference performance did not differ between the two block positions (-0.06; 95% CI: -0.63 to 0.48). There was, however, a credible condition × block interaction, and the pairwise contrasts below compare block 2 against block 1 separately within each learning condition. For material learned in the reading condition, inference performance was credibly higher in block 2 than in block 1 (1.03; 95% CI: 0.35 to 1.74). For material learned in the test-only condition (-0.30; 95% CI: -1.03 to 0.37) and for material learned by self-explaining (-0.54; 95% CI: -1.31 to 0.21), the differences were directionally negative and not credible. The improvement in the reading condition is credibly larger than the average improvement in the other two conditions (1.46; 95% CI: 0.88 to 2.05). All estimates are in log-odds, and the full model appears in Table 3.

As with Experiment 1, our exploratory analysis finds no overall benefit of an additional retrieval step on inference performance. However, in Experiment 2, such a benefit is now observed isolated to one condition, the reading condition, which had not been present in Experiment 1. When participants initially learned material by reading, there was a boost to inference test performance when it was preceded by fact questions. Of course, participants in the reading condition had the lowest overall performance on inference questions, so this boost should not be misinterpreted as yielding any practical advantage for the reading condition above the other two conditions.

Differences Between Learning Materials

We again examined differences between topics. Performance was highest for folktales, and in all three conditions performance on fact questions was worse for science texts than for folktales, inverting the pattern from the first learning block, when first-response accuracy was directionally highest for science questions. Our preregistered hypothesis that the fact advantage would be reduced for folktales was not confirmed. Our preregistered hypothesis that the selfexplaining advantage on inference questions would be reduced for science materials was confirmed. Full statistics appear in the Appendix.

Summary

In Experiment 2, we replicated and extended the findings from Experiment 1. All preregistered hypotheses were confirmed with only one minor exception.

On the delayed test, participants had better memory performance for facts that were learned in the test-only condition than facts that were learned by self-explaining or reading.

Again, this was expected, because participants in the test-only condition had been shown the fact questions and their correct answers during the learning phase.

For inference questions, which were novel to all participants, we observed better test performance when the material was learned by self-explaining than when it was learned in the test-only condition or reading. Inference questions required participants to make an inference between two facts, and in principle, someone who understood the constituent facts should have been able to correctly answer the inference questions. However, despite producing credibly better memory performance on the fact questions, the test-only condition had no advantage on inference questions. We observed no credible differences in inference performance between material learned in the test-only condition and material learned by reading. Only self-explaining yielded credibly better understanding on a delayed test than reading.

During the test phase, an additional step where participants attempted to retrieve fact questions did not credibly improve participants' subsequent performance on inference questions overall. However, such an improvement was observed in isolation, for material that had been learned in the reading condition. This improvement suggests that participants had encoded some fragmentary mental model of the passages in the reading condition's learning phase, and that these models became activated by the presentation of fact questions, consistent with prequestion effects (Motz et al., 2025). This prequestion effect may have been isolated to the reading condition because (a.) the test-only condition did not produce a coherent mental model in the first place, and (b.) to the extent possible, the self-explaining condition had already improved the encoding of relevant inferences from the passage. The latter possibility is explored in the Combined Analyses, below. Regardless, this finding leaves open the possibility that testonly learning can be involved in building broader understanding, but in the current study, this benefit was conditional on an initial reading exposure.

We again observe differences between learning materials. Test performance was lowest for science topics, despite participants showing higher initial accuracy on science fact questions during the learning phase. We again interpret this as evidence that participants' initial familiarity with science facts was isolated, and that they lacked the integrated understanding of science content to efficiently build new knowledge. There was no difference between fact and inference performance between the materials, and so we failed to confirm the preregistered hypothesis that the difference between fact and inference performance would be reduced for folktales (as had been observed in Experiment 1). However, we did confirm the preregistered hypothesis that the self-explaining advantage for inferences would be reduced for science topics. While participants showed a general tendency to have better inference performance on material that had been learned by self-explaining, this pattern was reduced for science texts.

Experiment 1 provided strong evidence that test-only learning with correct answer feedback was credibly less effective than self-explaining at facilitating broader understanding.

Experiment 2 replicated this effect, and further, Experiment 2 found no difference in inference performance between test-only learning with feedback and merely reading the passage. Selfexplaining yielded better inference performance than test-only and reading, even though learning in the test-only condition yielded credibly better fact memory than self-explaining and reading. How can participants learn facts so well in the test-only condition (better than self-explaining, and better than reading), but be unable to make accurate inferences between these facts? To begin to answer this question, we turn to the self-explaining condition.

In theory, the act of self-explanation biases participants to think about generalizable principles, invariant concepts, and causal relationships (Brockbank & Walker, 2022; Brockbank et al., 2023; Walker et al., 2014; Williams & Lombrozo, 2013). Specifically, this happens when the learner is generating their self-explanation; self-explanation causes the learner to build a better-integrated mental model at the time when they produce their self-explanation. The quality of a learner's generated self-explanation is a strong predictor of their later performance on knowledge assessments, and researchers generally view this as evidence the mental model that was activated during the process of self-explaining gets encoded during the learning phase (e.g., McNamara et al., 2024).

Extended to the current study, we might hypothesize that participants in the selfexplaining condition encoded a more comprehensive understanding during the learning phase, rather than constructing inferences from fact knowledge during the test phase. If this is true, we should expect to see the following patterns: (a.) Participants' performance on the inference test items will be predicted by whether their self-explanations during the learning phase include the key target inference, not by whether the self-explanations include the constituent facts; and (b.)

Participants will be less likely to generate key inferences when writing self-explanations about science texts. If these hypotheses are confirmed, it will provide evidence that participants are not making inferences during the test phase, and that broader understanding is actively assembled and encoded at the learning phase when participants generate self-explanations.

Combined Analyses

Experiments 1 and 2 demonstrated that self-explanation improved inference test performance overall. To investigate the mechanism underlying this benefit, we conducted two analyses on the self-explanations from participants in both experiments combined. First, we examined whether inference test performance is specifically predicted by making inferences during self-explanation, rather than by noting constituent facts, a pattern that would provide correlational evidence that the effectiveness of self-explanation depends on constructing inferences during learning, not merely encoding factual details. Second, because the selfexplaining advantage was diminished for science texts, we tested whether participants were less likely to generate key inferences when writing self-explanations about science texts compared to folktales and historical narratives.

Method

Participants and Self-Explanations

Participants in Experiment 1 could have been randomly assigned to the self-explanation condition for zero, one, two, or all three topics (folktale, history, and science passages). For the participants in Experiment 1 who were assigned to the self-explanation condition at least once (n = 216), they wrote self-explanations for 381 total passages. This means that participants in Experiment 1 who are included in the combined analysis were assigned to write selfexplanations for about 1.4 text passages on average. All participants in Experiment 2 (n = 68) wrote self-explanations for precisely one passage. All participants who were assigned to the self-explanation condition at least once in Experiment 1 (n = 216) and all participants in Experiment 2 (n = 68) are included in the current analysis, yielding a total of 284 participants providing self-explanations of 449 passages.

For any single passage, participants were prompted to write a self-explanation after each paragraph (see right panel of Figure 2). Passages included between 3 and 6 paragraphs; 4.2 paragraphs on average. For the current analysis, we only examine the final passing selfexplanation submitted to each prompt; self-explanations that did not meet our criteria are excluded. In effect, the combined analysis includes 1,984 passing self-explanations.

Coding

A trained research assistant evaluated each self-explanation and determined whether it included any direct mention of a relevant fact from one of the passage's corresponding fact questions, and whether it included any direct mention of an inference corresponding to one of the passage's inference questions. While coding, the research assistant was not aware of the study's hypotheses.

When a participant's self-explanation included a relevant fact, we refer to it as "noting"

the fact, since the relevant fact would have been stated explicitly in the passage. When a participant's self-explanation included a relevant inference, we refer to it as "making" an inference, because none of the inferences were explicitly stated in the passage; the inference would have been constructed by the participant.

A second trained research assistant used the same scheme to code 50 randomly sampled self-explanations from Experiment 1, blind to the codes applied by the first coder. To assess inter-rater reliability of the codes assigned to self-explanations, we used a proportion agreement method (Campbell et al., 2013; Miles and Huberman, 1984). This method divides the number of coding agreements (instances where both raters applied the same code) by the total number of code applications (all instances of agreement and disagreement) across all selfexplanations. This approach is particularly suitable for qualitative coding schemes where multiple codes are applied to a single response, and in the current analysis, 12 codes were applied to a single self-explanation (8 facts could be noted or not; 4 inferences could be generated or not). Agreement analysis revealed substantial consistency between coders, with an overall agreement of 94.3% (566 agreements out of 600 total codes applied), indicating strong reliability of the codes applied by the initial coder (Campbell et al., 2013).

Results and Discussion

For any given passage, there were 12 key elements that might be present in a participant's self-explanation: 8 relevant facts could be noted, and 4 relevant inferences could be made. Of these, on average, participants noted 5.4 facts per passage (68.0%; SD = 2.2) and made 2.4 inferences per passage (59.9%; SD = 1.3). These were not independent from one another, because the 4 relevant inferences each bridged 2 constituent relevant facts. Bivariate analysis finds that, overall, participants who note more facts during self-explanation also tend to make more inferences, r = 0.74 (95% CI: 0.69 to 0.78).

For each participant, we matched their responses to inference questions during the test phase with the relevant codes applied to their self-explanations in the learning phase. For each inference question, participants could have shown evidence of making the relevant inference in their self-explanation during the learning phase, and/or they could have noted one of the relevant facts (or both) in their self-explanation during the learning phase. We estimated a logistic mixed model where accuracy on inference questions was the outcome variable, and there were two fixed effects: fact noted (binary, either having noted one or both constituent facts; yes/no) and inference made (binary; yes/no). We also included a random intercept for each participant. This analysis revealed a strong credible effect of making an inference during the learning phase on a participant's accuracy on the corresponding inference question in the test phase (estimate in log-odds: 1.05; 95% CI: 0.66 to 1.44), and no credible effect of noting one or both corresponding facts (estimate: 0.12; 95% CI: -0.37 to 0.60). A direct comparison of the posterior estimates of these coefficients finds that, for answering inference questions on the delayed test, the effect of making an inference in the learning phase is credibly stronger than the effect of noting facts in the learning phase (0.93; 95% CI: 0.21 to 1.65, one-sided). As hypothesized, making the inference during self-explanation is a substantially stronger predictor of inference test performance than noting the constituent facts.

However, it remains possible that participants who are more engaged or attentive readers, or who differ on some other individual characteristic, are more likely to make inferences when writing self-explanations and that coincidentally, these participants are also more likely to answer inference questions correctly. If we want to provide support for the view that inferences during the learning phase have a selective advantage for inference performance during the test phase, we need to rule out the possibility that these could be jointly caused by a third variable.

To test this possibility, we examine participants' test performance on fact questions. If a third variable (such as a participant's general attentiveness or prior knowledge) causes participants to make inferences in their self-explanations and to perform well on inference questions at test, then we should expect that the presence of inferences in self-explanations would also be a strong predictor of participants' performance on fact questions on the delayed test. Alternatively, if making inferences in self-explanations has a selective advantage on inference performance during the test phase, then we should expect that noting facts in a self-explanation will be a better predictor of participants' performance on fact questions on the delayed test.

We ran the same logistic mixed model, but where accuracy on fact questions was the outcome variable, there were two fixed effects (whether a fact was noted or inference made in the corresponding self-explanations), and again, we included a random intercept for each participant. This analysis revealed a strong positive effect of noting facts in self-explanations for test performance on the fact questions (effect estimate in log-odds: 1.16; 95% CI: 0.86 to 1.49), and no credible effect of making an inference (estimate: 0.23; 95% CI: -0.08 to 0.54). Direct comparison of the posterior estimates finds that, for improving performance on fact questions, the effect of noting facts in the learning phase is credibly stronger than the effect of making inferences in the learning phase (0.94; 95% CI: 0.43 to 1.46, one-sided).

This double dissociation demonstrates that learners' behaviors during the learning phase predict performance in a domain-specific manner: Noting facts during self-explanation specifically predicts fact test performance, while making inferences predicts inference test performance. This minimizes the possibility that outcome test performance is driven by a common third variable (such as general attentiveness, prior knowledge, or motivation). Critically, each predictor shows a significantly stronger effect in its matched domain than the alternative predictor, with credible evidence for both contrasts.

In both Experiments 1 and 2, learning by self-explanation improved inference test performance overall, but this improvement was muted for science passages specifically. Given that making inferences during learning selectively predicted inference test performance, we next asked whether this muted benefit for science texts reflects participants making fewer inferences when self-explaining science texts.

We estimated the probability of observing the 12 key elements in participants' selfexplanations in a logistic mixed model, with element type (fact/inference) and topic (folktale/history/science) as fixed effects, and a random intercept for each participant. The main effect of element type was not credibly different from zero (estimate: 0.17; 95% CI: -0.06 to 0.41), and there were no credible differences in the overall probability of observing key elements between topics. However, for science texts specifically, there was a pronounced decrease in the relative likelihood of making an inference during the learning phase, illustrated in Figure 6.

Participants were credibly less likely to make an inference in their self-explanations of science Figure 6. Estimated probability of an element being present in a participant's self-explanation for the different topics. Up to 12 elements could be present in a participant's self-explanation: 8 relevant facts, and 4 relevant inferences. Bar height shows the estimated probability that elements would be observed in participant's self-explanations during the learning phase, and the error bars indicate the posterior estimate's 95% credible interval. Note that these are model estimates (with a random intercept controlling for each individual participant), so the bar heights differ slightly from the empirical probabilities.

texts than folktales (-1.82; 95% CI: -2.08 to -1.50) and historical narratives (-1.95; 95% CI: -2.24 to -1.65).

These combined analyses confirmed the two hypotheses: performance on the inference test items was strongly and selectively predicted by participants including the inferences in their self-explanations, and participants were less likely to generate these key inferences when writing self-explanations about science texts. These findings provide some evidence, albeit correlational, that the null effect of self-explanation on science texts can be explained by participants not making the relevant inferences during the learning phase. Moreover, these analyses provide complimentary evidence that merely encoding facts during learning will not lead to broader understanding. When participants learned by self-explaining, their accuracy on delayed inference test questions was not associated with their tendency to note key facts in their self-explanations; rather, their active assembly of inferences during the learning phase was the primary predictor of accurate responses to inference questions at test.

General Discussion

Our findings reveal a distinction in how different learning strategies shape knowledge representations in memory. Test-only learning builds what might be characterized as a collection of isolated nodes, efficiently accessible but poorly interconnected. This outcome persists even after retrieval practice. A second learning block in Experiment 2, in which participants successfully retrieved the answers they had encoded, had the effect of consolidating those isolated nodes, but did not help participants make connections between them. Self-explanation, by contrast, constructs an integrated network where facts exist not as discrete units but as elements within a coherent situation model (Kintsch, 1988; McNamara & Magliano, 2009; van Dijk & Kintsch, 1983; Zwaan & Radvansky, 1998; see also Rapp, Donovan, & Salovich, 2022).

This theoretical distinction challenges prevailing assumptions about the fungibility of learning strategies and suggests that test-only learning may be prioritizing memory strength at the cost of conceptual integration.

These findings speak directly to the theoretical tension between automatic activation and deliberate construction accounts of inference-making. If spreading activation were sufficient for building broader understanding (if retrieving fact A automatically strengthened connections to fact B in a way that enabled inference) then we should have observed a corresponding benefit from superior fact memory to inference performance in the test-only condition. We did not.

Participants who learned in the test-only condition showed superior fact retention yet performed no better on inference questions than those who merely read the material, despite explicit prompts to explain main ideas during the learning phase, and despite a 24-hour delay. These results data support the discourse comprehension perspective, which holds that inferences must be actively constructed and encoded during learning, not merely experienced as byproducts of co-activated concepts. The cognitive work happens during encoding, when learners notice gaps, generate bridging inferences, and integrate those inferences into their mental representation. These are processes that self-explanation facilitates and that test-only learning does not. Our findings are inconsistent with a spreading activation account that would predict correlated benefits across both question types, and they align with an account emphasizing distinct encoding processes for facts and for relational knowledge.

For assembling relational knowledge, testing may still have value when there is initial exposure to the material that facilitates initial construction of a situation model. If participants already have a mental model from prior exposure to material, testing may reactivate and strengthen that model (as observed in research on prequestions, Motz et al., 2025). This pattern was evident in our reading condition, where after initially reading the passages, a fact retrieval step during the test phase facilitated subsequent performance on inference questions (consistent with Eglington & Kang, 2018). Participants likely had encoded some fragmentary mental model of the passages during their initial reading, and these mental models became activated by the presentation of fact questions, enabling better inference performance.
