However, while this facilitation was statistically credible, reading alone was not, relatively speaking, an effective way to build broader understanding compared to self-explanation.

This picture aligns with Nguyen and McDaniel's (2016) results, in which a well-designed retrieval practice strategy including initial reading (the Read-Recite-Review or 3R method) failed to improve inference performance relative to note-taking. Inference benefits emerged only when 3R was supplemented with prompts instructing learners to make connections among ideas.

Consistent with our findings, retrieval strengthened memory for facts, but building a situation model required a different kind of cognitive activity during encoding.

We infer that testing may be most beneficial as a complementary strategy rather than a standalone approach. This is the complementarity that Roelle and colleagues (2022, 2023) theorize, with retrieval practice consolidating mental representations and generative activities constructing them. The current results show that when learning by testing alone, retrieval has only isolated facts to consolidate. When learners have access to rich initial exposures, whether through lectures, readings, or discussions, testing could serve to consolidate and reinforce the integrative connections. However, when learners skip these initial exposures and jump directly to self-testing, as is increasingly common in educational practice, they may be limiting themselves to surface-level memorization without developing the deeper understanding that would let them apply what they know to new situations. Ideally, learners should do both; selftesting and self-explanation should yield synergistic benefits when they are combined. This precise pattern was recently demonstrated by McCarthy and Hinze (2025), who found that explanation prompts combined with retrieval practice produced superior comprehension outcomes compared to either strategy alone (see also McNamara et al., 2023).

How do our findings comport with past claims that self-testing promotes transfer and application? We do not believe they are in conflict. Asher et al. (2025) concluded that practice with feedback alone yielded benefits for knowledge retrieval about statistical concepts, and for application questions that required participants to apply knowledge to novel situations. Their designs differ from ours in ways that bear directly on the boundary we are describing. In their studies, application questions were presented during the learning phase alongside fact questions, potentially allowing participants to memorize application-specific patterns rather than developing transferable understanding. Additionally, their application questions could often be answered correctly by restating definitions or applying memorized procedures (also the case in Larsen, Butler, & Roediger, 2013), which is a weaker test of broader understanding through inferential reasoning than the bridging inferences assessed here.

Similarly, the studies reviewed in Pan and Rickard's (2018) meta-analysis had highly variable implementations of self-testing, which sometimes included elements of self-explanation or elaborative feedback, and variable assessments of inference, where inference questions were sometimes included in the learning phase itself. The positive effects on inference learning reported in their meta-analysis are therefore what a consolidation account would predict. If a situation model had been assembled during initial exposure, retrieval had something to consolidate (e.g., Nguyen & McDaniel, 2016). Without that situation model, retrieval does not facilitate inferences.

Our combined analyses of participants' self-explanations provide insights into why selfexplanation facilitates broader understanding while test-only learning does not. The benefits of self-explanation appear to stem from the active construction of inferences during the learning phase itself, not from better encoding of constituent facts that might later be assembled into inferences. Converging evidence comes from Harders and Ebersbach (2026), who found no general effect of a self-explanation task on factual knowledge relative to rereading. Selfexplanation appears to yield benefits specifically when learners make inferences. When participants' self-explanations included explicit inferences connecting facts, they were substantially more likely to answer corresponding inference questions correctly on the delayed test. Critically, this relationship was selective: making inferences during self-explanation predicted inference test performance but not fact test performance, while noting facts during self-explanation predicted fact test performance but not inference performance. While correlational, this dissociation suggests that the cognitive activities engaged during selfexplanation will influence the resulting knowledge architecture, with inference-making during learning directly supporting later inference performance.

These findings support the theoretical proposition that comprehension depends critically on the construction of coherent situation models through active inferencing (Kintsch, 1988 (Kintsch, , 1998)). We observe no credible relationship between the number of key facts noted in the selfexplaining condition and participants' later performance on inference questions. Participants who demonstrated evidence of broader understanding in our study appeared to build situation models by making inferences when writing their self-explanations, going beyond the textbase level where propositional meaning is extracted from individual sentences. This aligns with theoretical accounts suggesting that self-explanation biases learners to think about generalizable principles, invariant concepts, and causal relationships (Brockbank & Walker, 2022; Brockbank et al., 2023; Walker et al., 2014; Williams & Lombrozo, 2013), such that the integrative processing occurs at the time of learning and the resulting enriched representation is what gets encoded into memory.

The benefits of self-explanation, however, are not universal. In our analyses by topic, we observed no credible advantage of self-explanation over test-only learning for science materials, despite participants showing higher initial accuracy on science fact questions. Participants appear to have entered the experiment with some familiarity with isolated science facts while lacking the integrated understanding that would support inference-making during encoding (Momsen et al., 2010). Consistent with this, participants were credibly less likely to generate inferences when self-explaining science texts than folktales and historical narratives, even though they mentioned key facts at comparable rates across all materials. It may be that the science texts in our study lacked narrative structure, temporal sequencing, and human characters, features that may naturally facilitate the construction of coherent situation models (Mandler, 1978; Thorndyke, 1977). Science texts also tend to be written with high cohesion and simpler syntax to compensate for unfamiliar concepts and vocabulary, which can remove the conceptual gaps that would otherwise prompt a learner to build a situation model (McNamara, 2013; McNamara, Graesser, & Louwerse, 2012). Readers with low knowledge of science are also less likely to gain from self-explanation unless they are provided with instruction and practice in comprehension strategies while self-explaining (McCarthy et al., 2018; McNamara, 2004 McNamara, , 2017)), which the current study lacked. However, these interpretations are speculative (see Appendix A). Future research might further examine whether presentations of science concepts that incorporate narrative elements or explicit structural scaffolds enable learners to build situation models more effectively during self-explanation (Soares et al., 2023; cf. Mengelkamp et al., 2025).

These findings have important theoretical implications for our understanding of the relations between memory and comprehension. Consistent with classic work demonstrating that memorization can occur without understanding (Chomsky, 1957; Moravcsik & Kintsch, 1993), our results show that robust memory for facts does not automatically lead to the ability to make inferences between those facts. This pattern is not unique to the current study. Ophuis-Cox et al. (2023) observed roughly the same dissociation in third-and fourth-grade classrooms using authentic course materials, suggesting that retrieval practice and explanation have differential effects on knowledge representations across age groups and learning contexts. This challenges implicit assumptions in much of the testing effect literature that improved memory for content will naturally support broader learning goals (see also Agarwal, 2018; Gonçalves et al., 2025; Jaeger et al., 2025). Instead, our findings suggest that different learning strategies engage distinct cognitive processes that lead to different types of knowledge representations: test-only learning promotes strong but isolated memory traces, while self-explanation promotes more integrated knowledge structures.

From a practical standpoint, these findings present a concerning picture for students or teachers who rely primarily on self-testing through digital flashcards and practice problems.

Such learners may be capably improving their knowledge of the tested material, but our results show that this is insufficient for building the broader understanding that supports flexible application of knowledge.

However, if the goal is simply to remember facts, test-only learning can be quite effective. Test-only learning was substantially more efficient than self-explaining, taking a median of 3.5 minutes per passage against 6.0 minutes in Experiment 1, and 3.6 minutes against 7.1 minutes in the first block of Experiment 2, while producing better memory for facts. For the goal of learning facts, testing alone is clearly an efficient choice. But for the broader goal of building understanding, self-testing should be preceded by or combined with learning experiences that support initial model construction, such as reading, lectures, or discussions. Such activities need not be passive, and they could be constructively augmented with activities like self-explanation. And if the material lacks features that facilitate a learner's generation of a situation model, as the science texts in the current study did, then additional instructional support will likely be necessary. Two further limitations concern the format of the testing condition. First, multiple-choice testing is one of the more constrained forms of retrieval. More generative formats, particularly free recall, impose reconstructive demands that may engage some of the same constructive processes we attribute here to self-explanation (see Roelle et al., 2023), so our conclusions are limited to the learning format we studied (multiple-choice questions). Second, the inferential demands of a multiple-choice item can be raised through the construction of its response options, and foils that require relational or inferential processing to reject can promote deeper encoding during retrieval (Little, Bjork, Bjork, & Angello, 2012). A test-only condition built with such foils might narrow the gap we observed. We should note, however, that processing an inferential lure presupposes a representation to process it against, which is what test-only exposure failed to supply. We would expect such a design to help most when testing follows some initial encounter with the material.

A related caution concerns the precision of Experiment 2. With 68 participants, its posterior credible intervals are roughly twice as wide as Experiment 1's. The interval for the crossover estimate spans 1.58 in log-odds in Experiment 2, but only 0.74 in Experiment 1, and a similar ratio holds across the other population-level effects (Tables 2 and 3). Smaller samples yield less precise posterior estimates, and the sensitivity analysis reported above indicates that Experiment 2 could only reliably detect crossovers of about 1.13 in log-odds or larger. We therefore read Experiment 2 as a preregistered confirmatory test that reproduced the direction and the pattern of results in Experiment 1, but we would not treat its point estimates as precise measurements of effect size.

The current research demonstrates that when testing with feedback is a learner's initial and primary encounter with material, it effectively promotes memory for specific facts and does not support the development of broader understanding, characterized by the ability to make inferences and connections between those facts. Furthermore, Experiment 2 demonstrates that adding retrieval practice to test-only learning did not change the outcome. Self-explanation, by promoting active inference construction during learning, yields better comprehension outcomes even when fact memory is weaker. These findings challenge the widespread advocacy for selftesting alone as a universally superior learning strategy and instead suggest a more nuanced view: different learning goals require different learning strategies. For educators and learners aiming to promote deep understanding rather than mere memorization, our results provide clear guidance that some assembly is required, and this assembly happens during learning through active explanation and inference-making, not merely through repeated retrieval of isolated facts.

In overall examinations of marginal means combining fact and inference questions and combining learning conditions, we observe credible differences between the materials. Learners had higher performance on folktales compared with historical narratives (0.72; 95% CI: 0.53 to 0.95) and compared with science texts (1.00; 95% CI: 0.79 to 1.20), and higher performance on historical narratives compared with science texts (estimate: 0.27; 95% CI: 0.10 to 0.47).

However, these differences are qualified by a significant three-way interaction between question type, condition, and topic, illustrated in Figure A1.

First, when participants learned in the test-only condition, test performance on fact questions was credibly worse for science texts compared with folktales (-1.16; 95% CI: -1.64 to -0.68) and compared with historical narratives (-0.94; 95% CI: -1.37 to -0.49). This is interesting and noteworthy, because it reflects an inversion in performance from the learning phase, where average accuracy on first response was highest for science texts, slightly worse for folktales, and lowest for historical narratives. We observe that the improvement in performance between the learning and test phases was credibly lower for science texts than folktales (-1.05; 95% CI: - 1.44 to -0.67) and historical narratives (-0.94; 95% CI: -1.31 to -0.57). There was no difference in learning gains between history and folktales. We might infer that the science facts were harder to learn and remember than the history and folktale facts, despite participants starting with greater prior knowledge about science.

Second, we find that when participants learned in the test-only condition, they perform credibly worse on inference questions compared with self-explanation (as previously discussed) for folktales (estimate: 1.33; 95% CI: 0.81 to 1.85) and historical narratives (estimate: 1.21; 95% CI: 0.82 to 1.72), but this difference does not apply to science topics (estimate: 0.07; 95% CI: -0.36 to 0.50).

There are multiple possible explanations for the lack of a difference for science topics, which are not mutually exclusive. For example, learning gains and memory test performance were worse for science facts, which likely hindered science inferences.

Additionally, participants' self-explanations for science texts were credibly shorter than their selfexplanations for folktales and historical narratives, possibly suggesting that participants made fewer inferences about science texts. This possibility is considered in depth in the Combined Analyses section.

Experiment 2

In Experiment 2 we again examine differences between the topics of the learning materials: folktales, historical narratives, and science texts. Isolating this analysis only to the first block of the test phase, we observe credible marginal mean differences between the topics. As was observed in Experiment 1, learners had higher performance on folktales compared with historical narratives (0.81; 95% CI: 0.37 to 1.2) and compared with science texts (0.85; 95% CI: 0.41 to 1.25), although in Experiment 2 there was no credible difference between historical narratives and science texts (estimate: 0.04; 95% CI: -0.43 to 0.37). However, these differences are qualified by credible interactions between question type, condition, and topic, illustrated in Figure A2.

In all three learning conditions (test-only, self-explaining, and reading), performance on fact questions was credibly worse for science texts compared with folktales, and this is again noteworthy because it inverts the pattern from the first block of the learning phase, when participants' first-response accuracy was directionally highest for science fact questions, suggesting some familiarity with the individual facts. We view this as mounting evidence that novel science facts were harder to learn and remember, despite participants' apparent familiarity with some of the individual science facts during the initial learning phase. This familiarity, reflected in higher initial accuracy on first responses, did not help students learn new facts, likely because recognizing an isolated fact is not the same as having the integrated situational knowledge that supports inference and understanding. Our preregistered hypothesis that the fact advantage (better performance on fact questions than inference questions) would be reduced for folktales (compared with the average of history and science topics) was not confirmed (estimate: -0.25; 95% CI: -1.57 to 1.13). In essence, participants had better performance on fact questions than inference questions across all materials (as described above). Furthermore, isolating the analysis to fact questions, there was no credible two-way condition × topic interaction; the conditions did not differ across topics in their effectiveness at improving memory for facts.

For inference questions, when participants learned via self-explanation, they performed credibly better than test-only and reading (as previously discussed), but we find that this effect is most pronounced for historical narratives. For historical narratives, participants who learned by self-explaining had credibly better inference test performance than test-only learning (estimate: 2.31; 95% CI: 0.99 to 3.72) and reading (estimate: 0.10; 95% CI: 1.04 to 3.46). There were directional advantages for self-explaining on inference questions for folktales and science texts, but these effects overlapped zero.

We also preregistered the hypothesis that the self-explaining advantage for inference questions (better performance on inference questions in the self-explaining condition compared with the average of the test-only and reading conditions) would be reduced for science materials. This hypothesis was confirmed; the self-explaining advantage on inference questions was credibly higher for folktale and history topics (on average) than science topics (estimate: 1.61; 95% CI: 0.13 to 3.17).

Comparing Test Performance Between Learning Conditions

When participants returned for the test phase, some participants were randomly assigned to the memory test block (where they answered fact questions; n = 39; 57.4%), while others were randomly assigned to the inference test block (where they answered inference questions; n = 29; 42.6%). Here, we isolate our analysis only to this first block of the test phase (either memory or inference), with full model statistics reported in Table 3, Model A.

Averaging across conditions, as was the case with Experiment 1, the log-odds of correctly answering a test question were higher for fact questions than for inference questions (estimate: 0.69; 95% CI: 0.15 to 1.2). entries are model coefficients, so each is expressed relative to the reference cell noted in parentheses. Each coefficient is a simple effect: main-effect rows give the contrast at the reference level of the other factor, and interaction rows give the difference between those simple effects. The comparisons reported in the main text are estimated marginal means and contrasts derived from these same posteriors, averaged over the remaining factor (and in places transformed to the response scale), so their values and signs will not correspond to individual coefficients in these tables.

questions was 93.0% (89.2 to 95.8%) for test-only, 78.6% (71.6 to 84.6%) for self-explaining, and 70.5% (62.6 to 77.8%) for reading; on inference questions, it was 67.4% (55.3 to 78.2%) for test-only, 83.4% (74.2 to 90.3%) for self-explaining, and 58.5% (46.0 to 70.7%) for reading.

When answering fact questions, participants had better performance for material that had been learned in the test-only condition than self-explaining (difference in proportion correct: 0.14; 95% CI: 0.08 to 0.20), and better performance than reading (estimate: 0.22; 95% CI: 0.15 to 0.29). This confirms the hypothesis that test-only learning would yield better memory for facts than self-explaining and reading. Fact question performance was marginally better for material learned by self-explaining than reading (estimate: 0.08; 95% CI: 0.01 to 0.15).

When answering inference questions, participants had credibly better performance for material that was learned by self-explaining than test-only learning (difference in proportion correct: 0.16; 95% CI: 0.05 to 0.28), and better performance than reading (estimate: 0.25; 95% CI: 0.14 to 0.38). This confirms the hypothesis that self-explaining would yield better inference performance than test-only and reading. There was no credible difference between test-only and reading conditions for inference questions (estimate: 0.09; 95% CI: -0.05 to 0.21), indicating that test-only learning is not reliably better than reading for causing learners to build broader understanding. As with Experiment 1, this is a remarkable finding, considering that when participants learned in the test-only condition, their memory for facts was much higher than the other two conditions. Even so, this superior fact knowledge did not yield improved performance on inference questions.

Appendix: Analyses of Differences Between Learning Materials

The passages used in the current study were selected to be educationally realistic rather than to be equivalent on the dimensions that are known to affect comprehension, and thus they were not controlled for length, cohesion, or elaboration (McNamara, Graesser, & Louwerse, 2012). Furthermore, to make inferences about the to-be-learned material, a reader needs to integrate the learning material with existing knowledge and construct a coherent representation (Albrecht & O'Brien, 1993). Thus, participants' ability to make inferences depends on the reader's prior knowledge about the material in front of them. Differences we observe between folktales, historical narratives, and science texts may therefore reflect any of these dimensions, or ordinary variation among passages, rather than the domain category itself. We reported these differences in the main text because they motivated the combined analyses of participants' self-explanations, where the operative variable is whether learners generated inferences during learning. The general conclusion from those analyses is that that when learners do not generate inferences while learning, their later inference performance suffers, and this holds regardless of the domain of the text.

We also examined test accuracy for each of the nine passages separately, pooling across learning conditions and restricting the analysis to the first test block, in order to compare learning performance between the passages (Table A1). For fact questions, differences between domains are larger than differences among passages within a domain (Experiment 1: betweendomain SD = .075, mean within-domain SD = .053; Experiment 2: .099 and .050). However, for inference questions, variability among passages within a domain is as large as variability between domains (Experiment 1: .076 and .077; Experiment 2: .087 and .096). Inference accuracy on the three historical narratives in Experiment 1, for example, ranged from .50 to .76, a spread wider than the range of the three domain means (.63 to .77), and the ordering of the three science passages is not stable across the two experiments. For these reasons, we preface these domain analyses with a statement that they are speculative. These analyses are at the level of the domain, but which specific passage a learner received accounts for about as much variation in inference performance as which domain it belonged to.

Interactions Between Condition and Learning Materials

Experiment 1

In the current study, we used three different forms of learning material: folktales, historical narratives, and science texts. Here we ask whether the differences between test-only and self-explaining vary between learning materials.

also offer a direct demonstration of the boundary. In learning from text, retrieval practice improved performance when the text was high in cohesion and elaboration, whereas generative activities improved performance when it was not. But what do learners build when testing is their only opportunity to construct a representation at all? As Ophuis-Cox et al. (2023) remarked, "it remains unclear if recalling factual knowledge contributes to understanding at a level that inferences can be made" (p. 3).

Figure 2.

Figure 2. Annotated screenshots of A. the test-only condition (left), and B. the self-explaining condition (right) during the learning phase.

Figure 3.

Figure 3. Performance during the test block immediately after the learning phase in Experiment 1. The heights of the error bars indicate the upper and lower bounds of the posterior's 95% credible interval of the cell mean. Semi-transparent points are individual participants' scores.

Figure 4.

Figure4. Experiment 2 procedure. During the learning phase (session 1), participants were randomly assigned to a topic order (Science, then History, then Folktale in the example shown), and the three conditions (test-only, self-explaining, or reading) were randomly assigned to topics. This learning phase was conducted in two identical blocks. During the test phase (session 2; minimum 24 hours after session 1), participants were randomly assigned to a block order; they could perform the memory test block first (as shown in the example above) or the inference test block first.

Figure 5.

Figure 5. Performance during the first block of the test phase in Experiment 2 (bars), alongside baseline performance (isolated error bars). Baseline performance for the fact questions is the mean accuracy of first response in the first block of the learning phase. Baseline performance for the inference questions is the mean accuracy from a secondary sample of untrained participants. The heights of the error bars indicate the upper and lower bounds of the posterior's 95% credible interval of the cell mean. Semi-transparent points are individual participants' scores.

These recommendations come with limits. The current research was conducted with volunteer research participants rather than students learning course material within an established curriculum. While this provided advantages in terms of experimental control and the ability to test across a variety of text passages, future research should explore whether these patterns hold in authentic educational settings, where learners are motivated to understand the material and experience real consequences for their performance. Furthermore, our study examined learning strategies in isolation, but in practice, successful students likely combine multiple strategies flexibly during self-regulated learning. Future work might also include selftesting conditions where learners can choose to also read a text and examine the extent to which students in natural learning environments self-direct efforts to build coherent understanding.

Figure A1.

Figure A1. Performance during test immediately after the learning phase in Experiment 1, split by question type and topic. Error bars indicate the upper and lower bounds of the posterior's 95% credible interval of the cell mean.

Figure A2.

Figure A2. Performance during the first block of the test phase in Experiment 2, split by condition, question type and topic. Error bars indicate the upper and lower bounds of the posterior's 95% credible interval of the cell mean.
